The Immersed Boundary Problem in 2-D: the Navier-Stokes Case
Jiajun Tong, Dongyi Wei

Abstract
. We study the immersed boundary problem in 2-D. It models a 1-D elastic closed string immersed and moving in a fluid that fills the entire plane, where the fluid motion is governed by the 2-D incompressible Navier-Stokes equation with a positive Reynolds number subject to a singular forcing exerted by the string. We introduce the notion of mild solutions to this system, and prove its existence, uniqueness, and optimal regularity estimates when the initial string configuration is C 1 C^{1} and satisfies the well-stretched condition and when the initial flow field u 0 u_{0} lies in L p ​ ( ℝ 2 ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) with p ∈ ( 2 , ∞ ) p\in(2,\infty) . A blow-up criterion is also established. When the Reynolds number is sent to zero, we show convergence in short time of the solution to that of the Stokes case of 2-D immersed boundary problem, with the optimal error estimates derived. We prove the energy law of the system when u 0 u_{0} additionally belongs to L 2 ​ ( ℝ 2 ) L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) . Lastly, we show that the solution is global when the initial data is sufficiently close to an equilibrium state.



1.
Introduction

In this paper, we consider the immersed boundary problem in 2-D with the incompressible Navier-Stokes equation.
It models a 1-D elastic closed string immersed and moving in a fluid that fills the entire
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The string applies elastic force to the ambient fluid and thus affects the flow field, and meanwhile, the flow moves and deforms the string. This gives an autonomous fluid-structure interaction problem, featuring singular forcing on the flow field along a time-varying 1-D string.
This problem arises from the general mathematical formulation of the immersed boundary problem introduced by Peskin
[
43
,
44
]
, and it is also closely related to the well-known numerical immersed boundary method he proposed, which has been widely applied as a power computational tool in physics, biology, medical sciences, and engineering over the past half a century
[
26
,
34
,
44
,
57
]
.
The primary goal of this paper is to present a comprehensive rigorous study of the problem, so as to put its mathematical formulation on the solid ground.

1.1.
Problem formulation

We model the 1-D elastic string by a planar Jordan curve.
We parameterize it by an
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-valued function
X=X(s,t)
, where
s\in\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
denotes the Lagrangian (material) coordinate, and where
t\geq 0
denotes the time.
Here
X(s,t)
gives the position of the material point along the string with the label
s
at time
t
, and we always assume that the parameterization goes counter-clockwise along the curve
X(\mathbb{T},t)
.
Note that
s
is not the arc-length parameter.
We shall use
u=u(x,t)
and
p=p(x,t)
to denote the velocity field and the pressure in the fluid, respectively.
Then the problem reads that
[
44
]

(1.1)

\displaystyle\rho(\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u)+\nabla p=\mu\Delta u+f_{X},

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\,u=0,\quad|u|,|p|\to 0\mbox{ as }|x|\to 0,

(1.3)

\displaystyle f_{X}(x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}F_{X}(s,t)\delta(x-X(s,t))\,ds,

(1.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}X(s,t)=u(X(s,t),t),

(1.5)

\displaystyle X(s,0)=X_{0}(s),\quad u(x,0)=u_{0}(x).

Here
\rho
and
\mu
are the density and dynamic viscosity of the fluid, respectively, which are assumed to be positive constants;

f_{X}(x,t)
is the elastic force applied to the fluid, which is singularly supported along the curve
X(\mathbb{T},t)
;
and
\delta
denotes the Dirac
\delta
-function in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
In (
1.3
),
F_{X}=F_{X}(s,t)
denotes the force density in the Lagrangian coordinate.
For simplicity, in this paper, we only study the case of the linear Hookean elasticity, i.e.,

(1.6)

F_{X}(s,t)=kX_{ss}(s,t),

where
k>0
denotes the Hooke’s constant, and where
X_{ss}
denotes the second derivative of
X
with respect to the
s
-variable.
In other words, each infinitesimal segment of the string is modeled as a Hooke’s spring with zero resting length.

Remark 1.1
.

To study general elasticity laws, one has
[
44
]

F_{X}(s,t)=\partial_{s}\left(\mathcal{T}(|X_{s}(s,t)|)\frac{X_{s}(s,t)}{|X_{s}(s,t)|}\right),

where
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}(q)
denotes the tension density of the string when an infinitesimal string segment is stretched by an amount of
q\in[0,+\infty)
.

\mathcal{T}(q)
is determined by the elastic energy density
\mathcal{E}(q)
of the string material,
\mathcal{T}(q)=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(q)
.
When an active elastic string is considered, e.g., in some biological models, one may assume spatially inhomogeneous and time-varying elasticity laws such as
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}(q,s,t)
.

1.2.
Non-dimensionalization

We first perform non-dimensionalization of the model to simplify the parameters.
Let
L_{*},T_{*},M_{*}>0
denote characteristic length, time, and mass scales respectively, which are to be chosen later.
We introduce dimensionless quantities as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{x},\,\tilde{t},\,\tilde{u},\,\tilde{p},\,\tilde{X},\,\tilde{f}_{\tilde{X}},\,\tilde{F}_{\tilde{X}},\,\tilde{\rho},\,\tilde{\mu},\tilde{k}\right)

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{x}{L_{*}},\,\frac{t}{T_{*}},\,\frac{u}{L_{*}/T_{*}},\,\frac{p}{M_{*}/T_{*}^{2}},\,\frac{X}{L_{*}},\,\frac{f_{X}}{M_{*}/(L_{*}T_{*}^{2})},\,\frac{F_{X}}{M_{*}L_{*}/T_{*}^{2}},\,\frac{\rho}{M_{*}/L_{*}^{2}},\,\frac{\mu}{M_{*}/T_{*}},\,\frac{k}{M_{*}/T_{*}^{2}}\right).

By a change of variable, one can derive from (
1.1
)-(
1.6
) a dimensionless system in the
(\tilde{x},s,\tilde{t})
-variable:

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(\partial_{\tilde{t}}\tilde{u}+\tilde{u}\cdot\nabla_{\tilde{x}}\tilde{u})+\nabla_{\tilde{x}}\tilde{p}=\tilde{\mu}\Delta_{\tilde{x}}\tilde{u}+\tilde{f}_{\tilde{X}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{\tilde{x}}\,\tilde{u}=0,\quad|\tilde{u}|,|\tilde{p}|\to 0\mbox{ as }|\tilde{x}|\to 0,

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\tilde{X}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{t})=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\tilde{k}\tilde{X}_{ss}(s,\tilde{t})\delta\big(\tilde{x}-\tilde{X}(s,\tilde{t})\big)\,ds,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tilde{t}}\tilde{X}(s,\tilde{t})=\tilde{u}\big(\tilde{X}(s,\tilde{t}),\tilde{t}\big),

\displaystyle\tilde{X}(s,0)=\tilde{X}_{0}(s),\quad\tilde{u}(\tilde{x},0)=\tilde{u}_{0}(\tilde{x}),

where

\tilde{X}_{0}(s):=L_{*}^{-1}X_{0}(s),\quad\tilde{u}_{0}(\tilde{x}):=T_{*}L_{*}^{-1}u_{0}(L_{*}\tilde{x}).

Now we define
L_{*}>0
by

\pi L_{*}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}X_{0}(s)\times X_{0}^{\prime}(s)\,ds,

where the right-hand side represents the area of the planar domain enclosed by the Jordan curve
X_{0}(\mathbb{T})
.
This definition is motivated by the fact that, if the system (
1.1
)-(
1.6
) admits a
well-behaved solution, the area of the domain enclosed by
X(\mathbb{T},t)
should be time-invariant because of the incompressibility of the flow.
We further choose
T_{*}:=\mu k^{-1}
and
M_{*}:=\mu^{2}k^{-1}
so that
\tilde{\mu}=\tilde{k}=1
.
Then

\tilde{\rho}=\frac{\rho}{M_{*}/L_{*}^{2}}=\frac{\rho k}{2\pi\mu^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}X_{0}(s)\times X_{0}^{\prime}(s)\,ds=:Re.

The dimensionless constant
Re
is exactly the Reynolds number.

In the rest of the paper, we will focus on the dimensionless system.
With the above choice of characteristic scales and with all the tilde symbols omitted for brevity, it can be summarized as

(1.7)

\displaystyle Re(\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u)+\nabla p=\Delta u+f_{X},

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\,u=0,\quad|u|,|p|\to 0\mbox{ as }|x|\to 0,

(1.9)

\displaystyle f_{X}(x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}X_{ss}(s,t)\delta(x-X(s,t))\,ds,

(1.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}X(s,t)=u(X(s,t),t),

and the initial condition is given by

(1.11)

X(s,0)=X_{0}(s),\quad u(x,0)=u_{0}(x).

Our goal is to prove the existence, uniqueness, and various other properties of its solutions.
The main results of the paper will be presented in Section
2.3
.
Readers are also referred to Section
1.4
for the organization of the paper.

1.3.
Related studies

As is mentioned before, the mathematical formulation of the immersed boundary problem and the numerical immersed boundary method was proposed in early 1970s
[
43
]
, and since then, the latter has been extensively investigated and widely applied (see e.g. the reviews
[
26
,
34
,
44
,
57
]
and the references therein).
However, rigorous analytic studies of the immersed boundary problem only started within the past decade.
So far, the existing literature focuses on the system (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) with
Re=0
, which is called the
Stokes case
of the 2-D immersed boundary problem, also known as the 2-D
Peskin problem
:

(1.12)

\displaystyle-\Delta u+\nabla p=f_{X},

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\,u=0,\quad|u|,|p|\to 0\mbox{ as }|x|\to 0,

(1.14)

\displaystyle f_{X}(x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}F_{X}(s,t)\delta(x-X(s,t))\,ds,

(1.15)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}X(s,t)=u(X(s,t),t),

(1.16)

\displaystyle X(s,0)=X_{0}(s).

What makes it mathematically more tractable than the general case is that, it can be reformulated into a contour dynamic equation for
X=X(s,t)
in the Lagrangian coordinate by virtue of the fundamental solution to the 2-D stationary Stokes equation (i.e., the 2-D Stokeslet)

(1.17)

G_{ij}(x):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(-\ln|x|\delta_{ij}+\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{|x|^{2}}\right),\quad i,j=1,2.

For instance, in the case
F_{X}=X_{ss}
, we can obtain that (see e.g.
[
31
,
38
]
for details)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}X=

\displaystyle\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}G(X(s,t)-X(s^{\prime},t))X_{ss}(s^{\prime},t)\,ds^{\prime}.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\frac{1}{4\pi}\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left[-\frac{|X^{\prime}(s^{\prime})|^{2}}{|X(s^{\prime})-X(s)|^{2}}+\frac{2((X(s^{\prime})-X(s))\cdot X^{\prime}(s^{\prime}))^{2}}{|X(s^{\prime})-X(s)|^{4}}\right]\big(X(s^{\prime})-X(s)\big)\,ds^{\prime}.

Here in the second line, for brevity, we wrote
X_{s}
as
X^{\prime}
and the
t
-dependence is omitted.
By extracting the principal singular part in the integral, we can rewrite it as a fractional heat equation

(1.18)

\partial_{t}X(s,t)=-\frac{1}{4}\Lambda X(s,t)+g_{X}(s,t),

where
\Lambda:=(-\Delta)^{1/2}_{\mathbb{T}}
, and where
g_{X}
is defined in (
2.11
) below.
One can show that
g_{X}
is in fact a regular forcing term, as long as
X
admits suitable regularity and satisfies
the well-stretched condition
— a string configuration
Y(s)
is said to satisfy the well-stretched condition (or simply,
Y(s)
is well-stretched) with constant
\lambda>0
, if

(1.19)

\big|Y(s_{1})-Y(s_{2})\big|\geq\lambda|s_{1}-s_{2}|_{\mathbb{T}},\quad\forall\,s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{T},

where
|\cdot|
on the left-hand side denotes the norm in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, while
|\cdot|_{\mathbb{T}}
on the right-hand side denotes the distance on
\mathbb{T}
.
Based on this idea, well-posedness of (
1.12
)-(
1.16
) with the linear Hookean elasticity
F_{X}=X_{ss}
was first proved in the independent works
[
31
]
and
[
38
]
for well-stretched initial data
X_{0}
that lies in the subcritical spaces
H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T})
and
h^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})
(the little Hölder space with
\gamma\in(0,1)
), respectively.
Besides, among other results,
[
38
]
also showed an instant smoothing property of the solution and a blow-up criterion.
Later, Rodenberg
[
50
]
extended the local well-posedness result in
[
38
]
to the case of general nonlinear elasticity.

After that, several well-posedness results have been obtained in scaling-critical regularity classes.
García-Juárez, Mori, and Strain
[
24
]
considered the case where the fluids in the interior and the exterior of the string can have different viscosities, and they proved global well-posedness for medium-size initial data in the critical Wiener space
\mathcal{F}^{1,1}(\mathbb{T})
.
In
[
21
]
, Gancedo, Granero-Belinchón, and Scrobogna introduced a toy model that captures the normal motion of the string, and its global well-posedness was proved for small initial data in the critical Lipschitz class
W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
.
Chen and Nguyen
[
9
]
proved the well-posedness in the critical space
\dot{B}_{\infty,\infty}^{1}(\mathbb{T})
, which contains non-Lipschitz functions.
For the 2-D Peskin problem with general nonlinear elasticity, Cameron and Strain
[
7
]
proved the local well-posedness in
B^{3/2}_{2,1}(\mathbb{T})
, while in a very recent work
[
25
]
, García-Juárez and Haziot obtained the global well-posedness and asymptotic stability for small initial data in some critical space which allows corners.

Some recent studies revealed special nonlinear properties of the 2-D Peskin problem.
In
[
55
]
, the first author of this paper introduced the tangential Peskin problem with a Eulerian formulation, which models an infinitely long and straight 1-D elastic string deforming tangentially in a Stokes flow in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Global solutions were constructed for initial datum in the energy class, which had been regarded as super-critical in the classic treatment using the Lagrangian formulation.
In our recent paper
[
56
]
on the original 2-D Peskin problem, we observed extremum principles for certain geometric quantities of the string, and then proved a global well-posedness result that only imposes a medium-size geometric condition on the initial string shape but no assumption on the size of its stretching.
Connection between the tangential problem and the dynamics of a circular string in the full model was also illustrated in this work.

In addition to those works, the first author of this paper studied the regularized Peskin problem inspired by numerical interests

[
54
]
.
Li
[
30
]
studied the case where the elastic string has both stretching and bending energy.
In
[
23
]
, García-Juárez, Kuo, Mori, and Strain considered the 3-D Peskin problem, where a 2-D closed elastic membrane moves in a 3-D fluid, and they proved its local well-posedness and smoothing of the solution.
In a very recent work
[
8
]
, Chen, Hu, and Nguyen obtained local well-posedness in critical spaces of the 2-D and 3-D Peskin problems with nonlinear elasticity as applications of their general regularity theory for quasilinear evolution equations.

Let us mention another two lines of recent research related to the Peskin problem.
It is natural to consider a 1-D elastic string moving in a 3-D Stokes flow.
However, in this case, the string velocity cannot be well-defined since the 3-D Stokeslet is too singular to be integrated along a 1-D curve.
Instead, one can consider the slender body problem where the 1-D string here is replaced by a filament which is a 3-D object with small constant cross-sectional radius; see the recent analytic works in
[
35
,
36
,
37
,
40
,
41
,
42
]
.
Besides, the tension determination problem was also studied recently as an inextensible version of the 2-D Peskin problem, where an inextensible closed string applies bending forces to the surrounding Stokes flow
[
22
,
28
]
.

The Peskin problem is known to be mathematically similar to several other evolution free boundary problems.
For instance, the Muskat problem
[
39
]
arising from the oil industry has attracted enormous attention in the analysis community over the past two decades; see e.g.
[
1
,
2
,
5
,
6
,
10
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
20
]
and the references therein.
Another one is the interface dynamics in a two-phase Stokes flow driven by the surface tension
[
3
,
4
,
11
,
32
,
33
,
49
]
.
When the two fluids have the same density and viscosity, this model can be viewed as (
1.12
)-(
1.16
) with
F_{X}(s,t)=\partial_{s}(X_{s}(s,t)/|X_{s}(s,t)|)
(if one ignores the diversity in the interface and domain geometry).
There is also a counterpart coming with the Navier-Stokes equation; see e.g.
[
18
,
19
,
27
,
46
,
47
,
48
,
49
,
51
]
.
In contrast to the Peskin problem, in these problems, the dynamics is solely governed by the shape of the interface while its parameterization is not intrinsic to the problem.

To the best of our knowledge, there has been no rigorous analysis result on the general 2-D immersed boundary problem (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) with the Navier-Stokes equation.

1.4.
Organization of the paper

The rest of this paper is structured as follows.
We define necessary notations in Section
2.1
, and introduce an important decomposition of the flow field in Section
2.2
.
After that, we will explain the key ideas of our analysis.
In Section
2.3
, we introduce the notion of the mild solution to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
), and present the main results of the paper.
A priori estimates for each part of the decomposition of the flow field will be established in Section
3
.
Using these estimates, we construct a local mild solution by a fixed-point argument in Section
4
.
Section
5
is devoted to studying the regularity and uniqueness of the mild solution.
In Section
5.1
, we derive sharp regularity estimates for the mild solutions in terms of some basic bounds of them.
Based on that, we address in Section
5.2
the uniqueness and continuation of the mild solution, and also prove a blow-up criterion.
In Section
5.3
, we further show that the solution enjoys even higher regularity.
In Section
6
, we justify the short-time convergence of the Navier-Stokes case to the Stokes case as
Re\to 0^{+}
, where the error estimates are derived with the optimal dependence on the Reynolds number.
In Section
7
, we prove the energy law of the system in the case that the initial flow field additionally satisfies
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Using that, in Section
8
, we show that, for initial data
(u_{0},X_{0})
that is sufficiently close to an equilibrium in a suitable sense, we can actually obtain a global solution.
We collect some parabolic estimates and simple calculus lemmas that will be used throughout the paper in Appendix
A
and Appendix
B
, respectively.
Lastly, in Appendix
C
, we present the proofs of several improved estimates for the nonlinear term
g_{X}
in the Stokes case (see (
1.18
)).

2.
Preliminaries and Main Results

2.1.
Definitions and notations

Throughout the paper, if not otherwise stated:

•

We will always use
s
and
s^{\prime}
to denote the Lagrangian coordinate that ranges in
\mathbb{T}
or its subsets;

•

x
and
y
will always denote the Eulerian coordinate that ranges in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or its subsets;

•

t
,
\tau
,
\eta
, etc., are used for the time variables;

•

For
X=X(s)
defined on
\mathbb{T}
or
X=X(s,t)
defined on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T]
, for brevity, we will use
X^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime\prime}
to denote
X_{s}
and
X_{ss}
, i.e., the first and second (partial) derivatives of
X
with respect to the
s
-variable, respectively;

•

\Lambda:=(-\Delta)^{1/2}_{\mathbb{T}}
.

Let us introduce some standard norms.

•

Let
u=u(x)
be defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\|u\|_{C_{x}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\nabla^{j}u\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\quad\|u\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\|\nabla^{k}u\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

We also denote

\|u\|_{C_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\|u\|_{C_{x}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|u\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\|u\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

With
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let

\|u\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\sup_{x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\atop x_{1}\neq x_{2}}\frac{|u(x_{1})-u(x_{2})|}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{\alpha}},

and for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define the Hölder semi-norms and Hölder norms as

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{k+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=

\displaystyle\;\|u\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\|\nabla^{k}u\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C_{x}^{k+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=

\displaystyle\;\|u\|_{C_{x}^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\nabla^{j}u\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|\nabla^{k}u\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

•

Similarly, for
X=X(s)
defined on
\mathbb{T}
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\|X\|_{C_{s}^{k}(\mathbb{T})}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|X^{(j)}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})},\quad\|X\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{k}(\mathbb{T})}:=\|X^{(k)}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}.

Here
X^{(j)}
denotes the
j
-th derivative of
X
with respect to
s
.
We also denote

\|X\|_{C_{s}(\mathbb{T})}:=\|X\|_{C_{s}^{0}(\mathbb{T})}=\|X\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{0}(\mathbb{T})}=\|X\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}.

With
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let

\|X\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}:=\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{T}\atop s_{1}\neq s_{2}}\frac{|X(s_{1})-X(s_{2})|}{|s_{1}-s_{2}|^{\alpha}_{\mathbb{T}}},

and for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\displaystyle\|X\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{k+\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}=

\displaystyle\;\|X\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}:=\|X^{(k)}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})},

\displaystyle\|X\|_{C_{s}^{k+\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}=\|X\|_{C_{s}^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}:=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|X^{(j)}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}+\|X\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}.

Here
|s_{1}-s_{2}|_{\mathbb{T}}
denotes the distance between
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
on
\mathbb{T}
, which ranges in
[0,\pi]
.
In what follows, we shall simply write it as
|\cdot|
when it incurs no confusion.

•

For
u=u(x,t)
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, we introduce the space-time norms: for
t\in(0,T]
, let

\|u\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}L_{x}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\sup_{\eta\in[0,t]}\|u(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Note that we took the supremum in time instead of the essential supremum in the usual definition of the
L^{\infty}
-norm.
For
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\|u\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}C_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
,
\|u\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
,
\|u\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}C_{x}^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, and
\|u\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}\dot{C}_{x}^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, etc., can be defined analogously.

•

For
X=X(s,t)
being a function on
\mathbb{T}\times[0,T]
, we can define
\|X\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}L^{p}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}
and
\|X\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}C^{\alpha}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}
, etc., in a similar way for
t\in(0,T]
.
We omit the details.

•

For
X=X(s)
and
Y=Y(s)
defined on
\mathbb{T}
, we denote

\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}:=\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}.

Similar quantities, such as
\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{C^{\alpha}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}
and
\|(u,v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, etc., can be defined analogously.

•

We introduce the Morrey norm on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
With
\mu\in(0,1)
, let

(2.1)

\|f\|_{M^{1,\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}:=\sup_{r>0,\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}r^{\mu-2}\|f\|_{L^{1}(B(x,r))},

where
B(x,r)
denotes a disk of radius
r
centered at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
By the Hölder’s inequality, we have
\|f\|_{M^{1,\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2/\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.

•

When the context is clear, we may omit
s
and/or
x
in the arguments and in the subscripts of the function spaces.
For instance,
\|X(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha}}
denotes
\|X(\cdot,\eta)\|_{C^{\alpha}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}
, while
\|u(\tau)\|_{L^{p}}
is short for
\|u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
, etc.

Next, we introduce notations that are specific to the analysis of the problem.

•

Throughout the paper, we denote
\nu:=Re^{-1}
.

•

Given
X=X(s,t)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, denote

(2.2)

d(x,t):=\inf_{s\in\mathbb{T}}{|x-X(s,t)|}.

•

Given
X=X(s)
, we follow
[
38
]
to denote

|X|_{*}:=\sup\{\lambda\geq 0:\,|X(s_{1})-X(s_{2})|\geq\lambda|s_{1}-s_{2}|_{\mathbb{T}}\mbox{ for all }s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{T}\}.

i.e., it is the largest constant
\lambda
such that
the well-stretched property (
1.19
) holds for
X
.
It is clear that, if
X\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T})
,
|X|_{*}\leq\inf_{s\in\mathbb{T}}|X^{\prime}(s)|\leq\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

•

If
X=X(s)
gives a
C^{1}
-Jordan curve in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
parameterized in the counterclockwise direction, we define its
effective radius

R>0
by

(2.3)

\pi R^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}X(s)\times X^{\prime}(s)\,ds.

Indeed, the integral here represents the area of the domain enclosed by the curve
X(\mathbb{T})
, so it is positive and thus
R
is well-defined.

•

For
X=X(s)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, we define the Fourier expansion of
X(s)
by

(2.4)

X(s)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\begin{pmatrix}\cos(ns)&-\sin(ns)\\
\sin(ns)&\cos(ns)\end{pmatrix}a_{n},

where for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the Fourier coefficients
a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
are defined by

(2.5)

a_{n}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\begin{pmatrix}\cos(ns)&\sin(ns)\\
-\sin(ns)&\cos(ns)\end{pmatrix}X(s)\,ds=:\mathcal{F}(X)_{n}.

We remark that, using the correspondence between complex numbers and vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we may rewrite (
2.4
) in the form of the more standard Fourier expansion of a complex-valued function:

X(s)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{ins}a_{n}
— in this formula, both
X(s)
and
a_{n}
should be interpreted as complex numbers.
However, to reduce confusion, we choose not to use this complex notation in this paper.

•

Assuming the expansion (
2.4
), we define a projection operator
\Pi
as in
[
38
, Equation (1.23)]
:

(2.6)

X^{*}(s):=a_{0}+\begin{pmatrix}\cos s&-\sin s\\
\sin s&\cos s\end{pmatrix}a_{1},\quad\Pi X(s):=X(s)-X^{*}(s).

It will be clear in Section
8
that, if
X=X(s)
represents a string configuration,
X^{*}(s)
is the closest equilibrium configuration to
X(s)
in the
L^{2}
-sense (not necessarily enclosing the same area), so the
\Pi X(s)
can be viewed as the deviation of
X(s)
from an equilibrium.

2.2.
Basic quantities and decomposition of the flow field

Let
G(x)
be the 2-D Stokeslet defined in (
1.17
).
Given
X=X(s,t)
, we define
u_{X}=u_{X}(x,t)
to be the Stokes flow in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
induced by the forcing
f_{X}
defined in (
1.9
), i.e.,

\displaystyle-\Delta u_{X}+\nabla p_{X}=f_{X},\quad\mathrm{div}\,u_{X}=0,

\displaystyle|u_{X}|,|p_{X}|\to 0\mbox{ as }|x|\to+\infty.

In other words,
u_{X}=\mathbb{P}(-\Delta)^{-1}f_{X}
, where
\mathbb{P}
denotes the Leray projection in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
It has been shown that
[
31
, Equations (2.1), (2.4) and (2.6)]

(2.7)

\begin{split}u_{X}(x,t)=&\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}G(x-X(s^{\prime},t))X^{\prime\prime}(s^{\prime},t)\,ds^{\prime}\\
=&\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}-\partial_{s^{\prime}}\big[G(x-X(s^{\prime},t))\big]\big(X^{\prime}(s^{\prime},t)-X^{\prime}(s_{x,t},t)\big)\,ds^{\prime}.\end{split}

Here
s_{x,t}\in\mathbb{T}
is taken such that

(2.8)

|x-X(s_{x,t},t)|=\inf_{s\in\mathbb{T}}|x-X(s,t)|.

If such
s_{x,t}
is not unique, take an arbitrary one; we shall omit the time variable and simply write
s_{x,t}
as
s_{x}
when it incurs no confusion.
It can also be justified that, for any given
c\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

[
31
, Equation (2.2)]
,

(2.9)

u_{X}(x,t)=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{T}}-\partial_{s^{\prime}}\big[G(x-X(s^{\prime},t))\big]\big(X^{\prime}(s^{\prime},t)-c\big)\,ds^{\prime}.

Taking
c=0
yields

[
31
, Equations (2.2) and (2.8)]

u_{X}(x,t)=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{T}}-\partial_{s^{\prime}}\big[G(x-X(s^{\prime},t))\big]X^{\prime}(s^{\prime},t)\,ds^{\prime}.

Denote

U_{X}(s,t):=u_{X}(X(s,t),t).

In the study of the Stokes case (e.g.
[
31
,
38
]
), it is split into two parts (also see (
1.18
))

(2.10)

U_{X}(s,t)=-\frac{1}{4}\Lambda X(s,t)+g_{X}(s,t),

where
\Lambda=(-\Delta)^{1/2}_{\mathbb{T}}
and

(2.11)

g_{X}(s,t):=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{T}}-\partial_{s^{\prime}}\big[G(X(s,t)-X(s^{\prime},t))\big]X^{\prime}(s^{\prime},t)\,ds^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}\Lambda X(s,t).

It is known that
g_{X}
can be regarded as a nonlinear remainder term that is more regular than
-\frac{1}{4}\Lambda X

[
31
,
38
]
; also see its estimates in Lemma
3.10
and Lemma
3.11
below.

Given
\nu=Re^{-1}>0
and
X=X(s,t)
, we
informally
let
u_{X}^{\nu}=u_{X}^{\nu}(x,t)
solve the linearized Navier-Stokes equation in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]
with zero initial data and forcing
f_{X}
defined in (
1.9
), i.e.,

(2.12)

Re\partial_{t}u_{X}^{\nu}-\Delta u_{X}^{\nu}+\nabla p_{X}^{\nu}=f_{X},\quad\mathrm{div}\,u_{X}^{\nu}=0,\quad u_{X}^{\nu}|_{t=0}=0.

Combining this with the definition of
u_{X}
, we find that

\partial_{t}u_{X}^{\nu}-\nu\Delta u_{X}^{\nu}=-\nu\Delta u_{X}.

Hence, we
rigorously
define
u_{X}^{\nu}
as follows:

(2.13)

\begin{split}u_{X}^{\nu}(t)=&\;-\nu\int_{0}^{t}\Delta e^{\nu(t-\tau)\Delta}u_{X(\tau)}\,d\tau\\
=&\;u_{X(t)}-e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{X(t)}-\nu\int_{0}^{t}\Delta e^{\nu(t-\tau)\Delta}(u_{X(\tau)}-u_{X(t)})\,d\tau\\
=:&\;u_{X(t)}-e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{X(t)}+h_{X}^{\nu}(t),\end{split}

where we used the identity
\nu\int_{0}^{t}\Delta e^{\nu(t-\tau)\Delta}w\,d\tau=e^{\nu t\Delta}w-w
with
w=u_{X(t)}
, and where we denoted

(2.14)

h_{X}^{\nu}(t):=-\nu\int_{0}^{t}\Delta e^{\nu(t-\tau)\Delta}(u_{X(\tau)}-u_{X(t)})\,d\tau.

For convenience, we also define

H_{X}^{\nu}(t):=h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\circ X(t).

Finally, given
u=u(x,t)
, we define

(2.15)

B_{\nu}[u](t):=-\int_{0}^{t}e^{\nu(t-\tau)\Delta}\mathbb{P}(u\cdot\nabla u)(\tau)\,d\tau.

Let us point out that
u_{X}
,
u_{X}^{\nu}
,
h_{X}^{\nu}
, and
B_{\nu}[u]
defined above are natural quantities to study.
In fact, if
(u,X)
is a sufficiently regular solution to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
), it is not difficult to verify the following important decomposition of
u
(see (
1.7
), (
2.12
), (
2.13
), and (
2.15
))

(2.16)

\begin{split}u(t)=&\;u_{X}^{\nu}(t)+e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{0}+B_{\nu}[u](t)\\
=&\;u_{X(t)}+h_{X}^{\nu}(t)+e^{\nu t\Delta}(u_{0}-u_{X(t)})+B_{\nu}[u](t).\end{split}

This decomposition reveals the structure of the flow field
u
, which inspires the analysis in the rest part of the paper.
Indeed, the first line of (
2.16
) shows that
u
consists of three parts:

(i)

u_{X}^{\nu}(t)
is fully determined by the dynamics of
X
up to time
t
, so its estimates only depend on
X
but not on
u
;

(ii)

e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{0}
is an explicit part coming from the initial flow field
u_{0}
, which is easy to control;

(iii)

and
B_{\nu}[u]
arises from the nonlinear term
u\cdot\nabla u
in the Navier-Stokes equation, which, due to the smoothing of the heat semigroup, should enjoy better regularity than
u
itself in the subcritical regime.

In the second line of (
2.16
), the flow field
u_{X}^{\nu}
obtained from solving the linearized Navier-Stokes equation is further decomposed into a purely Stokesian flow field
u_{X}
and the other two error terms (see (
2.13
)).
Combining (
2.16
) with (
1.10
) and (
2.10
), we find that
X
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}X=

\displaystyle\;u\circ X

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;-\frac{1}{4}\Lambda X+g_{X}+\big[h_{X}^{\nu}+e^{\nu t\Delta}(u_{0}-u_{X(t)})+B_{\nu}[u]\big]\circ X,

with the initial condition
X(s,0)=X_{0}(s)
.
As in the Stokes case (see (
1.18
)), this can also be treated as a fractional heat equation, which admits smoothing mechanism as well.
By the Duhamel’s formula,

(2.17)

\begin{split}X(s,t)=&\;e^{-\frac{t}{4}\Lambda}X_{0}(s)\\
&\;+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\frac{t-\tau}{4}\Lambda}\Big[g_{X}(\tau)+\big[h_{X}^{\nu}(\tau)+e^{\nu\tau\Delta}(u_{0}-u_{X(\tau)})+B_{\nu}[u](\tau)\big]\circ X(\tau)\Big]\,d\tau.\end{split}

In order to bound
X
, it suffices to show that
g_{X}
and
[h_{X}^{\nu}+e^{\nu t\Delta}(u_{0}-u_{X(t)})+B_{\nu}[u]]\circ X
have nice regularity estimates in terms of
u
and
X
.
This will be achieved in Section
3
.
In fact,

•

g_{X}
has been well-studied in the Stokes case, and yet we shall provide improved estimates for it;

•

while
[h_{X}^{\nu}+e^{\nu t\Delta}(u_{0}-u_{X(t)})+B_{\nu}[u]]\circ X
turns out to be more regular than
\Lambda X
and
g_{X}
under suitable conditions.

This eventually enables us to show the existence, uniqueness, and other nice properties of the solutions.

2.3.
Statement of the main results

Let us first introduce the notion of the mild solutions, which is directly motivated by the above representations (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) of
u
and
X
.

Definition 2.1
.

Assume that
u_{0}=u_{0}(x)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
p\in(2,\infty)
and
X_{0}=X_{0}(s)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T})
, which satisfy
\mathrm{div}\,u_{0}=0
and
|X_{0}|_{*}>0
.
For some
T\in(0,\infty)
,

(u,X)\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times C([0,T];C^{1}(\mathbb{T}))

is called a
(mild) solution
to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) on
[0,T]
, if the following holds:

(i)

\mathrm{div}\,u(x,t)=0
for any
t\in[0,T]
in the sense of distribution;

(ii)

\inf_{\eta\in[0,T]}|X(\eta)|_{*}>0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\frac{1}{p}}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}^{1/p}_{s}}<+\infty,

(iii)

and
(u,X)
satisfies that, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

(2.18)

\displaystyle u(t)=

\displaystyle\;u_{X}^{\nu}(t)+e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{0}+B_{\nu}[u](t),

(2.19)

\displaystyle X(t)=

\displaystyle\;e^{-\frac{t}{4}\Lambda}X_{0}+\mathcal{I}\big[W[u,X;\nu,u_{0}]\big](t),

where

(2.20)

W[u,X;\nu,u_{0}](t):=g_{X}(t)+\big[h_{X}^{\nu}+e^{\nu t\Delta}(u_{0}-u_{X(t)})+B_{\nu}[u]\big]\circ X(t),

and

(2.21)

\mathcal{I}[W](t):=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\frac{1}{4}(t-\tau)\Lambda}W(\tau)\,d\tau.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
u_{0}
and
X_{0}
be given as in Definition
2.1
.
For some
T_{*}\in(0,+\infty]
,
(u,X)\in L_{loc}^{\infty}([0,T_{*});L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times C_{loc}([0,T_{*});C^{1}(\mathbb{T}))
is said to be a
maximal (mild) solution
to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
), if the following holds:

(i)

for any
T<T_{*}
,
(u,X)
restricted on
[0,T]
gives a mild solution to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
2.1
;

(ii)

and
T_{*}
is maximal, which means either
T_{*}=+\infty
, or if
T_{*}<+\infty
, for any
T\geq T_{*}
, there is no mild solution to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) on
[0,T]
whose restriction on
[0,T_{*})
coincides with
(u,X)
.

We call
T_{*}
the
maximal lifespan
of
(u,X)
.
If
T_{*}=+\infty
, we say that
(u,X)
is a global (mild) solution.
If
T_{*}<+\infty
, we say a
finite-time singularity
occurs, or the solution
blows up
at the finite time
T_{*}
; in this case,
T_{*}
is referred as the
blow-up time
of
(u,X)
.

Our first theorem states that, for any initial data
(u_{0},X_{0})
satisfying the assumptions in Definition
2.1
, there exists a unique maximal solution to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
), and in short time, the solution can be quantitatively characterized in terms of the initial data.

Theorem 2.1

(Maximal mild solution and its short-time characterizations, Proposition
4.1
, Proposition
5.1
, and Corollary
5.3
)
.

Suppose that
u_{0}=u_{0}(x)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
p\in(2,\infty)
,
X_{0}=X_{0}(s)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T})
, and they satisfy that
\mathrm{div}\,u_{0}=0
and
|X_{0}|_{*}>0
.
Denote

(2.22)

M_{0}:=\|X_{0}^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})},\quad\lambda_{0}:=|X_{0}|_{*},\quad Q_{0}:=\|u_{0}\|_{L_{x}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\lambda_{0}^{-1+\frac{2}{p}}M_{0}^{2}.

Then there exists a unique
T_{*}\in(0,+\infty]
and a unique maximal mild solution
(u,X)
to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) with its maximal lifespan being
T_{*}
.
It holds that

(2.23)

T_{*}\geq C(p,\nu,\lambda_{0},Q_{0},M_{0},X_{0}),

where
C(p,\nu,\lambda_{0},Q_{0},M_{0},X_{0})
denotes a positive constant that depends on
p
,
\nu
,
\lambda_{0}
,
Q_{0}
,
M_{0}
, and
X_{0}
.
We will use similar notations in the sequel without further explanation.

In short time,
(u,X)
enjoys quantitative characterizations in terms of the initial data.
More precisely, there exists some
T>0
that depends on
p
,
\nu
,
\lambda_{0}
,
Q_{0}
,
M_{0}
, and
X_{0}
, such that:

(1)

u\in C_{loc}((0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and

\displaystyle\;\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\left(\lambda_{0}^{-1+\frac{2}{p}}M_{0}^{2}+Q_{0}\right),

where
C
is a constant only depending on
p
.

(2)

\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 2M_{0},\quad\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\frac{1}{p}}\|X^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1/p}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 4M_{0},

and

\inf_{t\in[0,T]}|X(t)|_{*}\geq\frac{\lambda_{0}}{2}.

(3)

X(s,t)\in C^{1}_{loc}((0,T)\times\mathbb{T})
, and it holds pointwise on
(0,T)\times\mathbb{T}
that

(2.24)

\partial_{t}X=-\frac{1}{4}\Lambda X+g_{X}+\big[h_{X}^{\nu}+e^{\nu t\Delta}(u_{0}-u_{X(t)})+B_{\nu}[u]\big]\circ X=u\big(X(s,t),t\big).

Besides,

\sup_{\eta\in(0,T)}\eta^{\frac{1}{p}}\|\partial_{t}X(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C(p,\nu,\lambda_{0},Q_{0},M_{0}).

Remark 2.1
.

It is well-known that, for the 2-D Navier-Stokes equation,
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the critical regularity for
u_{0}
, while in the Stokes case of the 2-D immersed boundary problem, the critical regularity for
X_{0}
is
\dot{C}^{1}(\mathbb{T})
and other scaling-equivalent regularity classes, such as
\dot{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
,
\dot{B}^{1}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
, and
\dot{H}^{3/2}(\mathbb{T})
, etc.
[
9
]
.
Hence, our results in Theorem
2.1
cover critical initial data for
X
and almost-critical initial data for
u
.

Since the
C^{1}_{s}(\mathbb{T})
-regularity is critical for
X
, it is conceivable that
T
, the length of the time interval on which we can quantitatively characterize the solution, depends on
X_{0}
itself in an implicit manner, but not merely on
M_{0}
and
\lambda_{0}
.
See Remark
4.3
and Remark
4.4
for more details.
Note that
T
here can serve as a lower bound for
T_{*}
, which implies (
2.23
).

The next theorem provides additional regularity estimates for the solution
(u,X)
in terms of some basic bounds of it.
Thanks to the definition of the mild solutions, these estimates apply to any compact time interval within the maximal lifespan of
(u,X)
.
They imply that, as
t
becomes positive, the solution will instantly gain higher regularity than what was stated in Theorem
2.1
.

Theorem 2.2

(Regularity of the solution, Lemma
5.1
, Lemma
5.2
, Lemma
5.3
, Lemma
5.5
, Lemma
5.6
, Proposition
5.4
, and Lemma
5.11
)
.

Let
p
and
(u_{0},X_{0})
be given as in Theorem
2.1
.
Suppose for some
T\in(0,\infty)
,
(u,X)
is a mild solution to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) on
[0,T]
in the sense of Definition
2.1
.
Denote

M:=\sup_{\eta\in[0,T]}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\frac{1}{p}}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}^{1/p}_{s}}<+\infty,

\lambda:=\inf_{\eta\in[0,T]}|X(\eta)|_{*}>0,

and

Q:=\sup_{\eta\in[0,T]}\|u(\eta)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}<+\infty.

In the above formulas, we omitted the
x
- and
s
-variables.
Then

(1)

u\in C([0,T];L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

(2)

In
(0,T)\times\mathbb{T}
,
\partial_{t}X(s,t)
is well-defined pointwise, and (
2.24
) holds pointwise.

(3)

\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,T)}\eta^{\frac{1}{p}}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C(T,p,\nu,\lambda,M,Q).

(4)

For any
\beta\in[0,1]
,

\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\beta}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{C^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C(T,p,\nu,\lambda,M,Q),

and for any
\beta\in[\frac{2}{p},1]
,

(2.25)

\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\beta}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{C^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C(T,p,\nu,\lambda,M,Q).

Note that the constants
C
do not depend on
\beta
.

(5)

For any
\beta\in(0,1)
,

\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{1+\beta}\|X^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\|_{C^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,T)}\eta^{1+\beta}\|\partial_{t}X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{C^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C(T,\beta,p,\nu,\lambda,M,Q).

(6)

For any
\beta\in(0,2)
,
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}t^{\beta}\|X^{\prime}(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T})}=0
.

(7)

For any
\beta\in[\frac{2}{p},1)
,

(2.26)

\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\beta}\|u(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(T,\beta,p,\nu,\lambda,M,Q),

and in addition, for any
0<t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T
,

t_{1}^{\beta}\|u(t_{1})-u(t_{2})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(T,\beta,p,\nu,\lambda,M,Q)|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\beta/2}.

(8)

For any
t\in(0,T]
,

\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(T,p,\nu,\lambda,M,Q)t^{-1}.

(9)

For any
t\in[0,T]
,

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}X(s,t)\times X^{\prime}(s,t)\,ds\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}X_{0}(s)\times X_{0}^{\prime}(s)\,ds,

i.e., the area of the region enclosed by the curve
X(\mathbb{T},t)
is constant in time.

Remark 2.2
.

These results are optimal in two ways.

•

The above quantitative bounds for
(u,X)
have the optimal dependence on
t
.
In fact, the time-singularity of the claimed estimates for
X
coincides with that of the estimates for the linear term
e^{-\frac{t}{4}\Lambda}X_{0}
, which in general cannot be improved in view of (
2.19
).
That in turn leads to the claimed time-singularity of
u
through (
2.18
).

We also remark that the condition
\beta\geq\frac{2}{p}
for (
2.25
) to hold is optimal as well.
Indeed, for
\beta\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\|e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq

\displaystyle\;C(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{p}-\frac{\beta}{2}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}e^{-t\Lambda}X_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}=

\displaystyle\;\|\Lambda e^{-t\Lambda}X_{0}\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq Ct^{-\beta}\|X_{0}^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}.

In view of (
2.18
), (
2.19
), and (
2.24
),
\|\partial_{t}X(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq Ct^{-\beta}
holds only when
\beta\geq\frac{1}{p}+\frac{\beta}{2}
, i.e.,
\beta\geq\frac{2}{p}
.
This also explains the range of
\beta
in (
2.26
).

•

On the other hand, one cannot expect the solution to enjoy even higher regularity than what is mentioned above.
In general, the flow field
u
can at most be Lipschitz in space, because the tangential force along the string gives rise to jump of
\nabla u
across the curve
X(\mathbb{T})

[
29
,
38
,
45
]
.
That also affects the spatial regularity of
X
through the term
B_{\nu}[u]
in (
2.19
) and (
2.20
).
As a result,
X
is not expected to be spatially smoother than
C^{2,1}_{s}(\mathbb{T})
in general.
See more detailed explanation in Remark
5.4
.

The next theorem is a blow-up criterion.

Theorem 2.3

(Blow-up criterion, Corollary
5.3
)
.

Let
p
,
(u_{0},X_{0})
,
(u,X)
, and
T_{*}
be given as in Theorem
2.1
.
If
T_{*}<+\infty
, at least one of the following scenarios would occur:

(a)

\limsup_{t\to T_{*}^{-}}\|u(t)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=+\infty
;

(b)

\liminf_{t\to T_{*}^{-}}|X(t)|_{*}=0
;

(c)

For any increasing sequence
t_{k}\to T_{*}^{-}
,
\{X^{\prime}(t_{k})\}_{k}
is not convergent in
C(\mathbb{T})
.
As a result,
X^{\prime}([0,T_{*}))
, which is the image of
[0,T_{*})
under the mapping
t\mapsto X^{\prime}(\cdot,t)
, is not pre-compact in
C(\mathbb{T})
.

As is mentioned in Section
1.3
, the Stokes case of the 2-D immersed boundary problem (
1.12
)-(
1.16
) has been studied extensively in the literature, which corresponds to the case of zero Reynolds number in (
1.7
)-(
1.11
).
It is natural to ask, as the Reynolds number
Re\to 0^{+}
, whether the solution to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) would converge in a suitable sense to the one to the Stokes case.
In the next theorem, we provide an affirmative answer to this question over a short time interval, and also bound the difference between the solutions in these two cases when
Re\ll 1
.
The theorem is stated in terms of the parameter
\nu=Re^{-1}
.

Theorem 2.4

(The zero-Reynolds-number limit, Proposition
6.1
, Proposition
6.2
and Remark
6.2
)
.

Let
p
and
(u_{0},X_{0})
be given as in Theorem
2.1
.
Define
M_{0}
,
\lambda_{0}
, and
Q_{0}
as in (
2.22
).

Take an arbitrary
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
There exists

T_{\dagger}=T_{\dagger}(\gamma,\lambda_{0},M_{0},X_{0})\in(0,1]\quad\mbox{and}\quad\nu_{*}=\nu_{*}(\gamma,p,\lambda_{0},M_{0},Q_{0})\geq 1,

such that the following holds for any
\nu\geq\nu_{*}
:

(1)

There exists a unique mild solution
X_{\dagger}=X_{\dagger}(s,t)
on
[0,T_{\dagger}]
to the Stokes case of the 2-D immersed boundary problem (
1.12
)-(
1.16
) (see the definition in Remark
6.1
), whose properties are established in Proposition
6.1
, and there exists a unique mild solution
(u_{\nu},X_{\nu})
on
[0,T_{\dagger}]
to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) with
Re=\nu^{-1}
, whose properties have been provided in Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.2
.

(2)

\{(u_{\nu},X_{\nu})\}_{\nu\geq\nu_{*}}
satisfies uniform-in-
\nu
bounds on
[0,T_{\dagger}]
:

\sup_{t\in[0,T_{\dagger}]}\|X_{\nu}^{\prime}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 3M_{0},\quad\sup_{t\in(0,T_{\dagger}]}t^{\gamma}\|X_{\nu}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C(\gamma,\lambda_{0},M_{0}),

\inf_{t\in[0,T_{\dagger}]}|X_{\nu}(t)|_{*}\geq\frac{\lambda_{0}}{2},

and

\|u_{\nu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{\dagger}}L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(p,\lambda_{0},M_{0},Q_{0}).

(3)

Moreover, for any
t\in(0,T_{\dagger}]
,

\|X_{\nu}(t)-X_{\dagger}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq Ct\cdot(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{p}},

\|(X_{\nu}-X_{\dagger})(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1}(\mathbb{T})}+t^{\gamma}\|(X_{\nu}-X_{\dagger})^{\prime}(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq Ct\cdot(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}},

and

\|u_{\nu}(t)-u_{X_{\dagger}(t)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\ln\nu\cdot t\|(X_{\nu}^{\prime}(t),X_{\dagger}^{\prime}(t))\|_{\dot{C}^{1}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\cdot(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{p}}+C(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{p}},

where the constants
C
depend on
\gamma
,
p
,
\lambda_{0}
,
M_{0}
, and
Q_{0}
.

Remark 2.3
.

This implies that, given
(u_{0},X_{0})
, for all sufficiently large
\nu
, the maximal lifespan of
(u_{\nu},X_{\nu})
has a uniform-in-
\nu
positive lower bound.

Remark 2.4
.

Let us highlight that the first two bounds for
X_{\nu}-X_{\dagger}
are optimal in terms of its
\nu
- and
t
-dependence.
Indeed, when compared with the dynamics of
X_{\dagger}
, the evolution of
X_{\nu}
is also affected by the initial flow field through the term
e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{0}
(see (
2.19
) and (
2.20
)).
It satisfies that

\|e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{p}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\quad\|\nabla e^{\nu t\Delta}u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Integrating this effect in time, we find that it leads to an error between
X_{\nu}
and
X_{\dagger}
in the exact form of the above bounds.
In a similar spirit, in the last bound for
u_{\nu}-u_{X_{\dagger}(t)}
, the
t
-dependence is also optimal, while the
\nu
-dependence is optimal up to a logarithmic factor.

If we additionally assume that
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we can prove the energy law for the system (
1.7
)-(
1.11
).

Theorem 2.5

(Energy law, Proposition
7.1
)
.

Let
p
,
(u_{0},X_{0})
,
(u,X)
, and
T_{*}
be given as in Theorem
2.1
.
We additionally assume that
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Then for any
t\in[0,T_{*})
,

\|X^{\prime}(t)\|_{L_{s}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\nu^{-1}\|u(t)\|_{L_{x}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}+2\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\,d\tau=\|X^{\prime}_{0}\|_{L_{s}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\nu^{-1}\|u_{0}\|_{L_{x}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

Remark 2.5
.

In view of this, we can define the total energy of the system as

E(t):=\frac{1}{2}\|X^{\prime}(\cdot,t)\|_{L_{s}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\nu}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L_{x}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

The first term is the Hookean elastic energy of the string, while the second term is the kinetic energy in the flow.
Theorem
2.5
implies that
E(t)
is non-increasing in
t
, and the energy loss is purely due to the viscous dissipation in the flow.

Next we prove that, when the initial data is small in a suitable sense, the maximal solution
(u,X)
to (
1.7
)-(
1.11
) is global.
The smallness of the initial data is quantified using its deviation from an equilibrium state.
We will see in Section
8
that the only equilibria of the system are in the form

u(x)\equiv 0,\quad X(s)=b_{0}+\begin{pmatrix}\cos s&-\sin s\\
\sin s&\cos s\end{pmatrix}b_{1}

for some
b_{0},b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
|b_{1}|>0
.
Note that such
X(s)
represents a circular and evenly-stretched string.

Theorem 2.6

(Global solution for small initial data, Proposition
8.1
)
.

Let
p
,
(u_{0},X_{0})
, and
(u,X)
be given as in Theorem
2.1
.
We additionally assume that
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and that
X_{0}=X_{0}(s)
is parameterized in the counterclockwise direction.
Let
R>0
be defined by

\pi R^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}X_{0}(s)\times X_{0}^{\prime}(s)\,ds.

Then there exists
\varepsilon_{*}>0
, which depends on
p
,
\nu
, and
R
, such that as long as

e_{0}:=\|\Pi X_{0}^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}+\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|u_{0}\|_{L^{q}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\varepsilon_{*},

(u,X)
is a global solution.
Here
\Pi X_{0}
was defined as in (
2.6
).

Moreover, it holds for all
t>0
that

\|u(t)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq CR^{1+\frac{2}{p}},

where
C
depends on
p
,

\|X^{\prime}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq CR,\quad|X(t)|_{*}\geq cR,

where
C>c>0
are universal constants, and

\|X^{\prime}(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{1/p}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C(p,\nu,R)\big(1+t^{-1/p}\big).

Lastly,

\|\Pi X^{\prime}(t)\|_{L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}+\nu^{-1/2}\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\big(1+\nu^{-1/2}\big)e_{0},

where
C
is universal.

Remark 2.6
.

Based on the above bounds, one can derive higher-order estimates for
(u,X)
by Theorem
2.2
.
However, we should point out that these bounds are not optimal as they fail to fully characterize the property that
(u,X)
stays
O(e_{0})
-close to equilibrium states, which we claim to be true.
To justify such refined estimates for
(u,X)
, we need more sophisticated analysis and lengthy arguments.
We choose to present that in a forthcoming work.

3.
Estimates for the Basic Quantities

This section is devoted to bounding the basic quantities in the decomposition of
u
which were introduced in Section
2.2
.
For convenience of the future use, we will summarize all the estimates in Section
3.1
, and then prove them in the subsequent subsections.

3.1.
Summary of the estimates

Let us start by introducing some notations.
Throughout this section, we shall always assume
\gamma
to be a real number in
(0,1)
.
Given
\lambda,T>0
, denote

O^{\lambda}:=\Big\{X=X(s)\in C^{1,\gamma}_{s}(\mathbb{T}):\,|X|_{*}\geq\lambda\Big\},

and

(3.1)

\begin{split}O_{T}^{\lambda}:=&\;\Big\{X=X(s,t)\in C([0,T];C^{1}(\mathbb{T})):\,|X(\cdot,t)|_{*}\geq\lambda\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T],\\
&\;\qquad\qquad\quad\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\gamma}\|X^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}<+\infty\Big\}.\end{split}

We note that the weight
\eta^{\gamma}
in time is motivated by the smoothing estimates of the fractional heat semi-group in (
2.19
) (also see Lemma
A.2
).

The first three lemmas aim at bounding
u_{X}
and
u_{X}^{\nu}
; see their definitions in Section
2.2
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
X\in O^{\lambda}
.

(1)

For any
t>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\lambda^{-1-\gamma}t^{-(2+k-\gamma)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}},

\|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L^{1}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\lambda^{-\gamma}t^{-(1+k-\gamma)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}},

where the constant
C
depends on
k
and
\gamma
, and

\|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\min\Big(\lambda^{-1}t^{-(2+k)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2},\;t^{-(3+k)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{2}}^{2}\Big),

\|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L^{1}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq Ct^{-(1+k)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{2}}^{2},

where
C
depends only on
k
.

(2)

For any
p\in(2,\infty)
,

\|u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\lambda^{-1+\frac{2}{p}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{2}}^{\frac{4}{p}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2-\frac{4}{p}},

where
C
depends only on
p
.

(3)

Moreover,

\lambda\|u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\lambda^{1+\gamma}\|u_{X}\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}},

where
C
depends only on
\gamma
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
X\in O_{T}^{\lambda}
.
Then for any
t\in(0,T]
,

\displaystyle\;\lambda\|u_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\lambda^{1+\gamma}\|u_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\lambda\|h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\lambda^{1+\gamma}\|h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;Ct^{-\gamma}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\gamma}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}},

where the constant
C
depends only on
\gamma
.

Moreover, for any
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\|u_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C(\nu t)^{\frac{1}{2p}}\lambda^{-1+\frac{1}{p}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{2}}^{\frac{2}{p}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2-\frac{2}{p}}.

If, additionally,
p\in(2,\infty)
,

\|u_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\lambda^{-1+\frac{2}{p}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{2}}^{\frac{4}{p}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2-\frac{4}{p}}.

Here the constants
C
’s depend only on
p
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
X\in O^{\lambda}_{T}
.
For any
0<t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T
,

\|u_{X}^{\nu}(t_{1})-u_{X}^{\nu}(t_{2})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\big(\nu|t_{1}-t_{2}|\big)^{\gamma/2}t_{1}^{-\gamma}\cdot\lambda^{-1-\gamma}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t_{2}}L_{s}^{\infty}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t_{2}]}\eta^{\gamma}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}},

where the constant
C
depends only on
\gamma
.

The next two lemmas bound
u_{X}-u_{Y}
and
u_{X}^{\nu}-u_{Y}^{\nu}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
X,Y\in O^{\lambda}
, and they satisfy
\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\leq\lambda/2
.
Then for any
t>0
, and any
\beta\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\;\big\|e^{t\Delta}(u_{X}-u_{Y})\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;Ct^{-\beta/2}\lambda^{-(1-\beta)}\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\;+Ct^{-(1-\gamma)/2}\lambda^{-1-\gamma}\big(\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\|X-Y\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}+\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2}\|X-Y\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\big),

where the constant
C
depends on
\gamma
and
\beta
.

Moreover, for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\;\big\|e^{t\Delta}(u_{X}-u_{Y})\big\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;Ct^{-\gamma/2}\lambda^{-1}\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\;+Ct^{-1/2}\lambda^{-1-\gamma}\big(\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\|X-Y\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}+\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2}\|X-Y\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\big),

where
C
depends on only
\gamma
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
X,Y\in O_{T}^{\lambda}
satisfy that
X(s,0)=Y(s,0)
and
\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L_{s}^{\infty}}\leq\lambda/2
.
Then, with
\mu_{0}\in[0,1)
, for any
t\in(0,T]
,

\displaystyle\;\|u_{X}^{\nu}(t)-u_{Y}^{\nu}(t)\|_{M^{1,1-\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\lambda^{-1-\gamma}\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\;\cdot\Big[t^{1-\gamma-\mu_{0}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\gamma}\|(X^{\prime}(\eta),Y^{\prime}(\eta))\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}\eta^{\mu_{0}}\|\partial_{t}(X-Y)(\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\;\quad+\|(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}_{s}}\Big],

where
C
is a constant depending on
\gamma
and
\mu_{0}
.
Here
\|\cdot\|_{M^{1,1-\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
denotes the Morrey norm; see the definition in Section
2.1
.

Although we have obtained some basic bounds for
h_{X}^{\nu}
(see its definition in (
2.14
)) in Lemma
3.2
, we will still need the following more refined estimate.

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose
X\in O_{T}^{\lambda}
, and it satisfies
\|X^{\prime}(t_{1})-X^{\prime}(t_{2})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\lambda/2
for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]
.

(1)

For
k=0,1
, if
\gamma\in(\frac{k}{2},1)
,
\gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\gamma]
, and
\mu_{0},\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in[0,1)
, we have that

(3.2)

\begin{split}&\;\|\nabla^{k}h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
\leq&\;C\left[(\nu t)^{-\frac{k}{2}}\min\left(\lambda^{-1},\,(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)+\mathds{1}_{\{\nu t\geq 4\lambda^{2}\}}(\nu t)^{-\gamma}\lambda^{-1-k+2\gamma}\right]t^{-\gamma(\mu-\gamma)}\\
&\;\cdot\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}\left(\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\gamma}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{C_{s}^{\gamma}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\left(\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\mu}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\right)^{\gamma}\\
&\;+C(\nu t)^{-\frac{1+k-\gamma^{\prime}}{2}}\min\left(\lambda^{-\gamma^{\prime}},\,(\nu t)^{-\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{2}}\right)\left[1+\mathds{1}_{\{\nu t\geq 4\lambda^{2}\}}\int_{2\lambda^{2}/(\nu t)}^{1}\zeta^{-\frac{1+k}{2}}\,d\zeta\right]\\
&\;\quad\cdot\lambda^{-1}\left(t^{1-\gamma^{\prime}-\mu_{0}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\gamma^{\prime}}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{C_{s}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\mu_{0}}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\right.\\
&\;\qquad\qquad\left.+t^{1-\mu^{\prime}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\mu^{\prime}}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}\right),\end{split}

where
C
depends on
k
,
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
,
\mu_{0}
,
\mu
, and
\mu^{\prime}
.

(2)

Again assume
\gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\gamma]
, and
\mu_{0},\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in[0,1)
.
For any
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,1+\gamma^{\prime}]
satisfying that
\alpha\leq 2\gamma
,

(3.3)

\begin{split}\|h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq&\;C\left[(\nu t)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\min\left(\lambda^{-1},\,(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)+\mathds{1}_{\{\nu t\geq 4\lambda^{2}\}}(\nu t)^{-\gamma}\lambda^{-1-\alpha+2\gamma}\right]t^{-\gamma(\mu-\gamma)}\\
&\;\cdot\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}\left(\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\gamma}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{C_{s}^{\gamma}}\right)^{1-\gamma}\left(\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\mu}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\right)^{\gamma}\\
&\;+C\left[(\nu t)^{-\frac{1+\alpha-\gamma^{\prime}}{2}}\min\left(\lambda^{-\gamma^{\prime}},\,(\nu t)^{-\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{2}}\right)\lambda^{-1}+\mathds{1}_{\{\nu t\geq 4\lambda^{2}\}}(\nu t)^{-1}\lambda^{-\alpha}\right]\\
&\;\quad\cdot\left(t^{1-\gamma^{\prime}-\mu_{0}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\gamma^{\prime}}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{C_{s}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\mu_{0}}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\right.\\
&\;\qquad\left.+t^{1-\mu^{\prime}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\mu^{\prime}}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}\right),\end{split}

where
C
depends on
\alpha
,
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
,
\mu_{0}
,
\mu
, and
\mu^{\prime}
.
Here if
\alpha>1
,
\|h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
is interpreted as
\|\nabla h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
.

(3)

Besides, for
H_{X}^{\nu}(t)=h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\circ X(t)
, if
\alpha\in(0,1]
,

\|H_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|X^{\prime}(t)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}^{\alpha},

while if
\alpha\in(1,2)
,

\displaystyle\|H_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{1,\alpha-1}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq

\displaystyle\;\|\nabla h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha-1}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|X^{\prime}(t)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}^{\alpha}+\|\nabla h_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|X^{\prime}(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\alpha-1}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}.

Lemma 3.7
.

Assume
T\leq 1
.
Suppose
X,Y\in O^{\lambda}_{T}
satisfy that
X(s,0)=Y(s,0)
, and that for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]
,

\|X^{\prime}(t_{1})-X^{\prime}(t_{2})\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\lambda/2,\quad\|Y^{\prime}(t_{1})-Y^{\prime}(t_{2})\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\lambda/2.

Denote

M:=\|X^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L_{s}^{\infty}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L_{s}^{\infty}}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\gamma}\|X^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,T]}\eta^{\gamma}\|Y^{\prime}(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})},

and, with some
\mu_{0}\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
and some
\beta\in[\gamma,1)
,

N_{0}:=\sup_{\eta\in(0,T)}\eta^{\mu_{0}}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}},\quad N_{\beta}:=\sup_{\eta\in(0,T)}\eta^{\beta}\|\partial_{t}X(\eta)\|_{C_{s}^{\beta}}.

Define

(3.4)

\begin{split}D(t)=&\;D[\gamma,\beta,X,Y](t)\\
:=&\;\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{s}^{\infty}}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\gamma}\|(X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime})(\eta)\|_{C_{s}^{\gamma}}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}\eta^{\beta}\|\partial_{t}(X-Y)(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\beta}},\end{split}

and

(3.5)

\begin{split}A(t)=&\;A[\gamma,\beta,\mu_{0},\nu,\lambda,X,Y](t)\\
:=&\;\nu^{-1}\left(D(t)+\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}\eta^{\mu_{0}}\|\partial_{t}(X-Y)(\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\right)\\
&\;\cdot\Big[\lambda^{-3}M^{3-\gamma}N_{\beta}^{\gamma}\big(1+\nu^{-1}t^{1-2\mu_{0}}N_{0}^{2}\big)+\lambda^{-1}M\\
&\;\quad+t^{(1-\gamma)/2-\mu_{0}}\cdot\lambda^{-2-\gamma}\nu^{-(1-\gamma)/2}M^{2}N_{0}\big(1+\nu^{-1}t^{1-2\mu_{0}}N_{0}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\\
&\;\quad+t^{(1-\gamma)/2-\mu_{0}}\cdot\lambda^{-1-\gamma}\nu^{-(1-\gamma)/2}M^{2}\Big].\end{split}

Then for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\|H_{X}^{\nu}(t)-H_{Y}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C\nu A(t),

and, if
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
,

\|H_{X}^{\nu}(t)-H_{Y}^{\nu}(t)\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{2\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C(\nu t)^{-\gamma}\nu M^{2\gamma}A(t).

Here the constants
C
’s depend on
\beta
and
\gamma
.

The next two lemmas collect all the estimates we need for
B_{\nu}[u]
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Given
u
, let
B_{\nu}[u]
be defined in (
2.15
).
The following estimates hold for any
t\in(0,T]
.

(1)

For
q\in(1,\infty]
and
r\in[1,q]
satisfying that
0<\frac{1}{r}<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{q}
,

(3.6)

\|B_{\nu}[u](t)\|_{L^{q}_{x}}\leq C\nu^{-1}(\nu t)^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{1-\frac{1}{r}}\|(u\otimes u)(\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{r}}.

Here we interpret
\frac{1}{\infty}
as
0
, and the constant
C
depends on
q
and
r
.
In particular, for
p\in(2,\infty)
,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\|B_{\nu}[u](t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\nu^{-1}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L_{x}^{2}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{p}},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\|B_{\nu}[u](t)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\nu^{-1}(\nu t)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{p}}\right)^{2},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\|B_{\nu}[u](t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\nu^{-1}(\nu t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{p}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}},

where the constants
C
’s depend on
p
.

(2)

For
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\|B_{\nu}[u](t)\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\nu^{-1}(\nu t)^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{(1+\gamma)/2}\|(u\otimes u)(\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{2/(1-\gamma)}},

where the constant
C
depends on
\gamma
.
Moreover, for
p\in[2,\infty)
,

(3.11)

\begin{split}&\;\|B_{\nu}[u](t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
\leq&\;C\nu^{-1}(\nu t)^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\left(\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\right)^{2},\end{split}

where the constant
C
depends on
p
and
\gamma
.

(3)

For
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
p\in[2,\infty)
,

(3.12)

\begin{split}&\;(\nu t)^{(2+\gamma)/2}\|B_{\nu}[u](t)\|_{\dot{C}^{1,\gamma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\nu t\|B_{\nu}[u](t)\|_{\dot{C}^{1}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
\leq&\;C\nu^{-1}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{1/2}\|u(\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\left[\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{p}}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{(1+\gamma)/2}\|u(\eta)\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}_{x}}\right],\end{split}

where the constant
C
depends on
p
and
\gamma
.

(4)

For
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

(3.13)

\begin{split}&\;(\nu t)^{\gamma/2}\|(B_{\nu}[u]-B_{\nu}[v])(t)\|_{M^{1,1-\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+(\nu t)^{1/2}\|(B_{\nu}[u]-B_{\nu}[v])(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
\leq&\;C\nu^{-1}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\gamma/2}\|(u-v)(\eta)\|_{M^{1,1-\gamma}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{1/2}\|(u(\eta),v(\eta))\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}},\end{split}

and

(3.14)

\begin{split}&\;(\nu t)^{(1+\gamma)/2}\|(B_{\nu}[u]-B_{\nu}[v])(t)\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\\
\leq&\;C\nu^{-1}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{\gamma/2}\|(u-v)(\eta)\|_{M^{1,1-\gamma}}\sup_{\eta\in(0,t]}(\nu\eta)^{1/2}\|(u(\eta),v(\eta))\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}.\end{split}

Here the constants
C
’s depend only on
\gamma
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
p\in[2,\infty)
.
Then for any
0<t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T
,

\displaystyle\;\|B_{\nu}[u](t_{2})-B_{\nu}[u](t_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\nu^{-1}\big(\nu(t_{2}-t_{1})\big)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\nu t_{1})^{-\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\left(\sup_{\eta\in(0,t_{1}]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,t_{1}]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\;+C\nu^{-1}\big(\nu(t_{2}-t_{1})\big)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\nu t_{2})^{-\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\left(\sup_{\eta\in(0,t_{2}]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}+\sup_{\eta\in(0,t_{2}]}(\nu\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u(\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\right)^{2},

where the constant
C
depends on
p
and
\gamma
.

Proofs of these lemmas will be presented in the rest of this section.

Finally, we recall the nonlinear term
g_{X}
defined in (
2.11
).
Its Hölder estimates have been studied in several existing works; see e.g.
[
38
, Propositions 3.8, 3.9, 3.12]
and
[
8
, Lemma 7.15]
.
Nevertheless, in order to derive optimal regularity estimates for the solutions, we will need the following improved estimates, which are of independent interest.

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose
X\in O^{\lambda}
.
Let
g_{X}
be defined in (
2.11
).
Then

\|g_{X}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C\lambda^{-2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}.

If
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
,

\|g_{X}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{2\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C\lambda^{-2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}^{2},

and if
\gamma\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
,

\|g_{X}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{1,2\gamma-1}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C\lambda^{-3}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}^{2}.

Here
C
’s are constants that only depend on
\gamma
.

We highlight that the Hölder estimates above have exactly quadratic dependence on
\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}
, which is crucial for deriving the regularity estimates for
(u,X)
in Section
5
with the optimal time-singularity at
t=0
.

In a similar spirit, we also have that

Lemma 3.11
.

Suppose
X,Y\in O^{\lambda}
.
Let
g_{X}
and
g_{Y}
be defined in (
2.11
) in terms of
X
and
Y
, respectively.
Then

\displaystyle\|g_{X}-g_{Y}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\lambda^{-2}\big(\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\;\cdot\Big[\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}+\lambda^{-1}\big(\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\big)\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\Big].

If
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
,

\displaystyle\|g_{X}-g_{Y}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{2\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\lambda^{-2}\big(\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\big)\big(\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\big)

\displaystyle\;\cdot\Big[\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}+\lambda^{-1}\big(\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\big)\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\Big].

If
\gamma\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
,

\displaystyle\|(g_{X}-g_{Y})^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{2\gamma-1}(\mathbb{T})}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\lambda^{-3}\big(\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\big)^{2}\big(\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\big)

\displaystyle\;\cdot\Big[\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}+\lambda^{-1}\big(\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}+\|Y^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\big)\|X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\Big].

Here
C
’s are constants that only depend on
\gamma
.

The above two lemmas can be justified as the existing estimates in
[
8
,
38
]
.
One only has to carefully track the dependence on various norms of
X
,
Y
, and
X-Y
.
Nevertheless, for completeness, we still provide self-consistent new proofs in Appendix
C
, which are of independent interest.

3.2.
Estimates for
u_{X}
and
u_{X}^{\nu}

By the definition of
u_{X}
(see (
2.9
) and also (
2.13
)),

(3.15)

-\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}K(x-X(s),t)X^{\prime\prime}(s)\,ds=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{s}[K(x-X(s))](X^{\prime}(s)-c)\,ds,

where
c\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is arbitrary.
Here
K
denotes the fundamental solution of the linearized Navier-Stokes equation in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

K(x,t):=\frac{e^{-|x|^{2}/(4t)}}{4\pi t}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{x\otimes x}{|x|^{2}}\right)-\frac{1-e^{-|x|^{2}/(4t)}}{2\pi|x|^{2}}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{2x\otimes x}{|x|^{2}}\right).

It satisfies the following estimates.

Lemma 3.12
.

K(x,t)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,+\infty))
, and for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

|\nabla_{x}^{k}K(x,t)|\leq C(t+|x|^{2})^{-(2+k)/2}.

Moreover,

|\nabla_{x}^{k}K(x,t)-\nabla_{y}^{k}K(y,t)|\leq C|x-y|\left((t+|x|^{2})^{-(3+k)/2}+(t+|y|^{2})^{-(3+k)/2}\right).

Here the constants
C
’s only depend on
k
.

Proof.

For
z\in(0,+\infty)
, let

\varphi(z):=e^{-z}-\frac{1-e^{-z}}{2z},\quad\psi(z):=\frac{1}{z}\left(e^{-z}-\frac{1-e^{-z}}{z}\right)=-\frac{1-e^{-z}(1+z)}{z^{2}}=\frac{d}{dz}\left(\frac{1-e^{-z}}{z}\right).

We additionally define
\varphi(0)=1/2
and
\psi(0)=-1/2
.
It is not difficult to show by induction that, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
z>0
,

\left(\frac{d}{dz}\right)^{k}\left(\frac{1-e^{-z}}{z}\right)=(-1)^{k}\cdot\frac{k!}{z^{k+1}}\left[1-e^{-z}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{z^{j}}{j!}\right].

As a result,

•

\varphi(z),\psi(z)\in C^{\infty}([0,+\infty))
;

•

and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
z\geq 0
,

|\partial_{z}^{k}\varphi(z)|\leq C_{k}(1+z)^{-1-k},\quad|\partial_{z}^{k}\psi(z)|\leq C_{k}(1+z)^{-2-k},

where
C_{k}
’s are universal constants depending on
k
.

With these notations,

K(x,t)=\frac{\varphi(|x|^{2}/(4t))}{4\pi t}\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\psi(|x|^{2}/(4t))}{\pi(4t)^{2}}x\otimes x.

Then the desired claims follow from induction and direct calculation.
∎

The following two lemmas will be useful in the calculation below.

Lemma 3.13
.

Suppose
|X|_{*}\geq\lambda
. Let
d(x)
and
s_{x}
be defined as in (
2.2
) and (
2.8
), respectively.
Then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{T}
,

(3.16)

|x-X(s)|\geq\max\left\{d(x),\,\frac{\lambda}{2}|s_{x}-s|_{\mathbb{T}}\right\}.

Proof.

It follows from
[
31
, Equation (A.1)]
and the definition of
d(x)
.
∎

Lemma 3.14
.

Suppose
|X|_{*}\geq\lambda
.
For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
t>0
, and
m>1/2
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{ds}{(t+|x-X(s)|^{2})^{m}}\leq C\min(\lambda^{-1}t^{1/2-m},\,t^{-m}),

where the constant
C
depends only on
m
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
3.13
and direct calculation.
∎

Now we are ready to bound
u_{X}
and
u_{X}^{\nu}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

By (
3.15
), for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.17)

\begin{split}-\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}(x)_{i}=&\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}X_{l}^{\prime}(s)\partial_{l}\nabla^{k}K_{ij}(x-X(s),t)(X^{\prime}(s)-X^{\prime}(s_{x}))_{j}\,ds\\
=&\;\int_{\mathbb{T}}X_{l}^{\prime}(s)\partial_{l}\nabla^{k}K_{ij}(x-X(s),t)X_{j}^{\prime}(s)\,ds.\end{split}

Here and in what follows, we adopt the Einstein summation convention.

From the first representation, we can derive with Lemma
3.12
and Lemma
3.13
(also see Lemma
3.14
) that, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.18)

\begin{split}|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}(x)|\leq&\;C\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{|X^{\prime}(s)||X^{\prime}(s)-X^{\prime}(s_{x})|}{(t+|x-X(s)|^{2})^{(3+k)/2}}\,ds\leq C\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}|s-s_{x}|^{\gamma}}{(t+\lambda^{2}|s-s_{x}|^{2})^{(3+k)/2}}\,ds\\
\leq&\;C\lambda^{-1-\gamma}t^{-(2+k-\gamma)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}},\end{split}

where the constant
C
depends on
k
and
\gamma
.
On the other hand, (
3.18
) also gives

|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}(x)|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\lambda^{-\gamma}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}}{(t+|x-X(s)|^{2})^{(3+k-\gamma)/2}}\,ds.

Hence,

\|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\lambda^{-\gamma}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}}{(t+|x|^{2})^{(3+k-\gamma)/2}}\,dx\leq C\lambda^{-\gamma}t^{-(1+k-\gamma)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}},

where
C
also depends on
k
and
\gamma
.

From the second representation in (
3.17
), we similarly argue that

\displaystyle|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}(x)|\leq

\displaystyle\;C\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{|X^{\prime}(s)|^{2}}{(t+|x-X(s)|^{2})^{(3+k)/2}}\,ds\leq C\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{|X^{\prime}(s)|^{2}}{(t+\lambda^{2}|s-s_{x}|^{2})^{(3+k)/2}}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\min\big(\lambda^{-1}t^{-(2+k)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2},\,t^{-(3+k)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{2}}^{2}\big),

where the constant
C
only depends on
k
.
On the other hand, integrating this on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
yields that

\|\nabla^{k}\Delta e^{t\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{T}}|X^{\prime}(s)|^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{(t+|x|^{2})^{(3+k)/2}}\,dx\right)ds\leq Ct^{-(1+k)/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{2}}^{2},

where the constant
C
only depends on
k
as well.

To prove the estimates for
u_{X}
, we observe that

(3.19)

u_{X}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\Delta e^{\tau\Delta}u_{X}\,d\tau.

Hence, with
X\in O^{\lambda}
,

\displaystyle\|u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq

\displaystyle\;\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\Delta e^{\tau\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\int_{0}^{\infty}\min(\lambda^{-1-\gamma}\tau^{-(2-\gamma)/2},\,\tau^{-3/2})\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\lambda^{-1}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}},

where the constant
C
depends on
\gamma
.
For any
p\in(2,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\|u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq

\displaystyle\;\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\Delta e^{\tau\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\Delta e^{\tau\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{\frac{1}{p}}\|\Delta e^{\tau\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\tau^{-1/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{2}}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{p}}\left[\min\big(\lambda^{-1}\tau^{-1}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2},\,\tau^{-3/2}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{2}}^{2}\big)\right]^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\lambda^{-1+\frac{2}{p}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{2}}^{\frac{4}{p}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}}^{2-\frac{4}{p}},

where the constant
C
depends on
p
.
Finally, applying Lemma
3.15
below with
\varphi\equiv 1
, we also have that

\displaystyle\|u_{X}\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\left|\sup_{\tau>0}\tau^{(2-\gamma)/2}\|\Delta e^{\tau\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right|^{1-\gamma}\left|\sup_{\tau>0}\tau^{(3-\gamma)/2}\|\nabla\Delta e^{\tau\Delta}u_{X}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right|^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\lambda^{-1-\gamma}\|X^{\prime}\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\gamma}}\|X^{\prime}\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}},

where the constant
C
depends on
\gamma
.

This completes the proof.
∎

In the proof above, we used the following lemma on Hölder estimate of an integral term.

Lemma 3.15
.

Assume that
\varphi:(0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
is decreasing on
(0,+\infty)
and it is integrable on any bounded interval.
Then for
\alpha\in(0,1)
and any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}v(x,\tau)\,d\tau\right\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq

\displaystyle\;\frac{C}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau\left|\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}(t-\eta)^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}\varphi(\eta)^{-1}\|v(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right|^{1-\alpha}

\displaystyle\;\cdot\left|\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}(t-\eta)^{\frac{3-\alpha}{2}}\varphi(\eta)^{-1}\|\nabla v(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right|^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}V(s,\tau)\,d\tau\right\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq

\displaystyle\;\frac{C}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau\left|\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}(t-\eta)^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}\varphi(\eta)^{-1}\|V(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\right|^{1-\alpha}

\displaystyle\;\cdot\left|\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}(t-\eta)^{\frac{3-\alpha}{2}}\varphi(\eta)^{-1}\|\partial_{s}V(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\right|^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{t}V(s,\tau)\,d\tau\right\|_{\dot{C}_{s}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq

\displaystyle\;\frac{C}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau\left|\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}(t-\eta)^{1-\alpha}\varphi(\eta)^{-1}\|V(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\right|^{1-\alpha}

\displaystyle\;\cdot\left|\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}(t-\eta)^{2-\alpha}\varphi(\eta)^{-1}\|\partial_{s}V(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{s}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\right|^{\alpha},

where the constants
C
’s only depend on
\alpha
.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}v(x,\tau)\,d\tau\right\|_{\dot{C}_{x}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\left|\sup_{\eta\in(0,\infty)}\eta^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}\|v(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right|^{1-\alpha}\left|\sup_{\eta\in(0,\infty)}\eta^{\frac{3-\alpha}{2}}\|\nabla v(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right|^{\alpha},

where
C
only depends on
\alpha
.
We also have other two estimates that are parallel to those above.

Proof.

Denote

\displaystyle A:=

\displaystyle\;\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}(t-\eta)^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}\varphi(\eta)^{-1}\|v(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

\displaystyle B:=

\displaystyle\;\sup_{\eta\in(0,t)}(t-\eta)^{\frac{3-\alpha}{2}}\varphi(\eta)^{-1}\|\nabla v(\cdot,\eta)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\tau\in(0,t)
,

\displaystyle|v(x,\tau)-v(y,\tau)|\leq

\displaystyle\;\min(2\|v(\tau)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\,|x-y|\|\nabla v(\tau)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\min\left(2(t-\tau)^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}\varphi(\tau)A,\,|x-y|(t-\tau)^{\frac{\alpha-3}{2}}\varphi(\tau)B\right).

Hence, with
\varphi
being decreasing,

\displaystyle\;\left|\int_{0}^{t}v(x,\tau)\,d\tau-\int_{0}^{t}v(y,\tau)\,d\tau\right|\leq\int_{0}^{t}|v(x,\tau)-v(y,\tau)|\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;\int_{0}^{t}\min\left(2(t-\tau)^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}A,\,|x-y|(t-\tau)^{\frac{\alpha-3}{2}}B\right)\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\int_{0}^{t/2}\min\left(t^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}A,\,|x-y|t^{\frac{\alpha-3}{2}}B\right)\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau

\displaystyle\;+C\int_{t/2}^{t}\min\left((t-\tau)^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}A,\,|x-y|(t-\tau)^{\frac{\alpha-3}{2}}B\right)\varphi\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;C\left[\varphi\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t/2}\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau\right]A^{1-\alpha}\big(|x-y|B\big)^{\alpha}.

This implies the desired inequality, since given the monotonicity of
\varphi
,

\varphi\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t/2}\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau\leq\frac{3}{t}\int_{0}^{t/2}\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau\leq\frac{3}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\tau)\,d\tau.

The other claims can be justified similarly.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

By (
2.13
) and Lemma
3.1
,

\displaystyle\;\|u_{X}^{\nu}(t)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq
